A process of the server's user is the Steward's when, walking up its parents, the first deciding fact says so: a reaper (pid 1, `launchd`, `init`, `systemd`) or the server's own tree (itself, its ancestors, its process group) is nobody's; a member of a process group the supervisor started **in this server run** (`ProcessSupervisor.agentGroups()`: one per spawn, restart or resume, pgid = the CLI's pid, members started within the leader's lifetime as this run saw it — from the fork to the moment its `exit` fired, not after the drain and reap — never a PID-table row of an earlier run, whose pgid may since name the user's processes) is that agent's; a child of one of the Steward's is the Steward's (escaped with `setsid`); an orphan (parent a reaper), or a process below an orphan of the Steward's, whose identity (pid, start time) the server **remembered** is the Steward's (`why: remembered`, no marker needed): while a Steward's CLI runs, `StewardProcesses` records every process taken through its groups (by the two rules before) — on every list and stop and 750 ms after each `agent.tool_call`/`agent.idle` of the Steward, in memory for this server run, at most 2048 per Steward, forgotten once a snapshot no longer shows it — because Claude Code runs each Bash command in a process group of its own and macOS `ps -E` hides the environment of Apple binaries (`/bin/sh`, `/bin/zsh`), so a tool command's shell orphaned when the CLI died carries no readable marker; otherwise an orphan is the Steward's only when **all** of: the Steward has a task worktree (`…/.galactic/worktrees/<task>`), the orphan started inside one of the Steward's sessions (`agentSessions()`: each spawn or resume to its end, ±1 s for `lstart`, plus an earlier run's latest row of this boot to its recorded end — captured before this run's first spawn of the agent overwrites it, so a resumed Steward keeps it), its working directory is inside that worktree, and its environment carries the Steward's marker — an unreadable environment is no marker; below such an orphan every process needs the marker (or to be remembered) too; neither orphan rule ever takes a **user-facing app** — a command inside a macOS `*.app/Contents/` bundle (`code .` → Electron, terminal emulators) or a terminal multiplexer server (`tmux`, `screen`, `zellij`, `dtach`, `abduco`) — nor anything below one, because what the user later does in it (other folders, integrated terminals, panes) inherits the marker; anything else (a child of the user's shell, of the server, of another agent, a daemon the user started in the worktree) is not. `runtime/steward-processes.ts` (`StewardProcesses`, built by `composeRuntime` from `ComposeOptions.processes`, `index.ts` passes the supervisor) lists them — its own CLI left out while the Steward runs, and what that CLI uses (its direct children: MCP servers, the shell of a tool command, and their descendants) marked `held` — and stops them: every requested pid must be attributable now (else nothing is signalled, `DomainError('invalid')`); the attributed descendants go too, except held ones not named themselves; "stop all" (no pids) only for a Steward that ended; a target that started in the snapshot's own second is re-attributed after that second (and skipped if still that young); right before SIGTERM the table is read again and a pid whose start time or parent changed is skipped; nothing is signalled once the service is disposed (shutdown), checked after each wait; SIGTERM + SIGCONT to each pid (never a group), SIGKILL after `termMs` (3 s) to the ones whose pid *and* start time are unchanged; `agent.processes_stopped{agentId, pids, commands (clipped, redacted), forced?, by, sweep?}` is logged and `StopProcessesResponse.skipped` lists what was left alone. The **sweep** (a log subscriber) stops what is attributable when a Steward ends (`finished`/`failed`/`stopped`, after `whenExited` — the supervisor's own group reap — or 15 s) and, for every ended Steward of a task, when the task becomes `completed`/`failed`/`archived`; a live Steward is never swept (checked again right before the signals, against a resume), and neither is an interrupted one (shutdown: it comes back). Exposed as `GET /api/agents/:id/processes` and `POST /api/agents/:id/processes/stop {pids?}` (bearer-guarded; 404 unknown agent, 400 not a Steward, a pid that is not its, or no pids for a running Steward; the panel sends exactly the pids it showed after reading the list again) and as the brains' `list_steward_processes` / `stop_steward_processes` tools (§5.3). Residual risk: `lstart` has one-second resolution and the last identity read precedes the signal by a few milliseconds, so a pid recycled within that window *and* within the same second, with the same parent, is not detected; pid reuse on macOS and Linux cycles through the whole pid space first, so this needs a wrap-around inside that window. Further residual risks (notes): on macOS `ps -E` prints the environment space-separated, so a variable whose value contains ` GALACTIC_AGENT_ID=<this Steward's id>` — or an argv rewritten between the two `ps` reads to end that way — reads as a marker; a Steward that exports its own marker into the worktree's `.envrc`/`mise.toml` marks the user's later shells there once the user allows the file.
- **Settings** (P25-01, SPEC decision 51): `config/settings.ts` (`SettingsService`) reads the effective settings, validates a patch as a whole, writes `config.json` atomically (read fresh, changed keys merged, checked with `ConfigFile`, written to a 0600 temporary file and renamed — the token and every other key kept) and applies live what it can: the global default table (`setGlobalDefaultPolicy`), retention (`Archival.retentionMs`), caps (`CapsEnforcer.update`; running agents keep theirs), transcript retention (`TranscriptRetention.policy`, P8-02), the budget warning threshold (the `BudgetWatcher` re-judges every budget, P8-01) and the tier; the projects root and `stewardsLoadUserSettings` need a restart (`restartRequired`). An environment-decided setting (`envOverrides`: `GALACTIC_PROJECTS_ROOT`, `GALACTIC_RETENTION_DAYS`, any `GALACTIC_MAX_*` locks `caps`, `GALACTIC_BUDGET_WARN_PERCENT` locks `budgets`) is refused with 409 `env_override`. The projects root is saved cleaned (trimmed, `~` expanded, normalised) and refused with `Workspaces`' own rule (`unsafeFolder` on the `canonical`, symlink-free path: `/`, the home directory itself, inside or containing the data directory — `dataDir`, the config's and the database's folders).

### 2.3 Desktop shell: optional Tauri 2 app (P8-06)

Because the server runs as a normal local process and the UI is a web app, the simplest v1 is `galactic start` → opens `http://localhost:4321`. If you want a "real app" with a dock icon, tray status and auto-start, wrap the *same* web UI in **Tauri 2** (small binaries, Rust core, mobile targets exist). Electron is the fallback if you ever need Node inside the shell itself, but you don't: the server is already a separate process, and Electron's size/memory cost buys nothing here.

**The shell (P8-06; `apps/desktop`, `docs/DESKTOP.md`, notes `docs/tasks/notes/P8-06-desktop.md`).** Tauri 2.12 (crates `tauri` 2.12.1, `tauri-plugin-notification` 2.5.1, `tauri-plugin-autostart` 2.7.0; CLI `@tauri-apps/cli` 2.12.1; all Apache-2.0 OR MIT). macOS first (`tauri build --bundles app`, unsigned / ad-hoc signed). It does not bundle the server: it runs the repository it was built from (`GALACTIC_REPO` overrides) with the user's Node (`GALACTIC_NODE`, else the login shell's `node`, else Homebrew / Volta / nvm), and gives that Node the login shell's PATH so agents see the user's tools. Two halves:

- **Rust** (`src-tauri/src/main.rs`): the window, the menu bar icon, OS notifications, the login item. It starts the Node side (`apps/desktop/dist/main.js`) with piped stdio and renders what it says. Line protocol (`apps/desktop/src/lines.ts`): Node → Rust JSON lines `server{url, port, owned}`, `status{state, waiting, storage, view}`, `notify{id, title, body}`, `phone{on, label, url?, detail?}` (P9-16), `menu{menu: {permissions: [{id, version, title, sending?}], permissionCount, questions: [{id, title}], questionCount}}` (P9-15), `exited`; Rust → Node `quit` / `start` (one word a line) and, from the menu, one JSON object a line `{"type":"answer","questionId","version","decision":"allow"|"deny"}` (P9-15; anything else is ignored); stdin closing counts as `quit`. No line carries the token; the launcher's banner line with the token is redacted before it reaches the log (`~/Library/Logs/dev.galacticagents.desktop/desktop.log`, `GALACTIC_DESKTOP_LOG`).
- **Node** (`lifecycle.ts`, `supervisor.ts`, `watcher.ts`): the server's lifecycle and the status. **Attach or start**: the data lock (§6.4, P8-03) names the holder; a live server holder whose `/health` answers is attached to; a starting holder or an import / restore is waited for; none (or a dead pid) → `galactic start --no-open [--port]` in its own process group. Whether a lock is stale is left to the server: a start the lock refuses ("is using") goes back to attaching, and a live-pid holder that does not answer for 15 s gets such a start. Its own server crashing is started again (≤ 3 in 5 min); an attached server that goes away is **not** replaced (the user stopped or is restarting it), the menu offers "Start Mission Control". **Quit** stops only a server this shell started: SIGTERM to the launcher (forwarded to the server, which stops its agents), SIGKILL to the launcher's group after 35 s; an attached server is never signalled. The shell itself dying closes the Node side's stdin, which then stops what it started. **Status**: one read-only WebSocket session (token from `GALACTIC_TOKEN` or `config.json`, sent only in `hello`), folded with the protocol's `applySnapshot` / `applyEvent`: the page's "waiting for you" count, the storage condition (P8-09), the connection.

**Security model unchanged.** The window loads the splash page (`tauri://localhost`) and then `http://127.0.0.1:<port>`, the server's own origin: the page gets its token from `/health` (WKWebView sends `Sec-Fetch-Site`) and opens its socket exactly as in Safari; nothing is injected but a flag. No IPC capability is granted to any page (`app.security.capabilities: []`, no app commands; the plugins are called from Rust only), so the server's page has no bridge into the shell and the shell adds no route into the server. Navigation stays on the shell's pages and that one origin; other http(s) links open in the default browser.

**Menu bar**: the status (loudest first: starting / disconnected / not running / error, then a storage problem, then "N waiting for you", then "Live"; the count or "!" next to the icon), which Mission Control and who started it, Open, Start Mission Control (when stopped), **Start at Login** (`tauri-plugin-autostart`, a LaunchAgent, off by default, started with `--hidden`: no window, menu bar only), Quit (says when it stops Mission Control). Closing the window hides it; the Dock icon reopens it.

**Permission requests in the menu bar** (P9-15; SPEC decision 136; `apps/desktop/src/tray-menu.ts`, `answer.ts`, `src-tauri/src/tray_menu.rs`). The Node side builds the menu's question sections from its folded world on every snapshot and event and sends a `menu` line when they change (empty unless the status socket is live): the permission questions that are open and held by the user, oldest first, each with its `version`, and the clarification questions the user holds; at most 10 of each, plus the totals. Titles are "<agent>: <tool> <summary>" (the summary is the input's `command` / `file_path` / `path` / `url` / … or its JSON), made safe on the Node side — one line, redacted by a menu-local shape pass on top of the server's own redaction of the logged call (tokens, `name=value` secrets, `--password x`, URL credentials, bearer headers, key prefixes, JWTs, long opaque strings), `\p{Cc}` / `\p{Cf}` (bidi overrides and isolates, zero-width) removed, capped at 80 characters with "…" — and again on the Rust side (controls and bidi removed, capped, `&` doubled: muda reads a single `&` as a mnemonic on macOS). Rust rebuilds the tray menu on each `menu` line: "Permission requests (N)" with one submenu per request ("Allow once", "Deny", "Open in GalacticAgents"; ids `allow:<version>:<id>`, `deny:…`, `focus:<id>`), "N more — open the app", "Questions (N)" whose items open the app. A click on Allow once / Deny writes an `answer` command; the Node side answers through the panel's route, `POST /api/questions/:id/answer` with `Authorization: Bearer <token>` and `{answer: "allow" | "no", version, answerId: "tray:<uuid>"}` — the panel's own answers, never an "always" (none exists in the route, and the menu has none). 409 `stale` (answered elsewhere, or the version moved) → a notification "Permission request already answered" with the server's "already answered by …"; 404 / other refusals / no server → a notification to answer it in the app; success → nothing (the stream updates the menu and the panel). Only permission questions are answered from the menu (a clarification id is refused locally); a second click while one answer is in flight is ignored and the item shows "sending…". "Open in GalacticAgents" shows the window and navigates it to `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/#focus=question:<id>` — the push clicks' fragment; the page reads it at load and now on `hashchange` too (`apps/web/src/pwa/notification-clicks.ts`). No IPC is added (`capabilities: []`): the answer goes Rust → Node (stdin) → server, never through the page. The Node side logs only "menu answer (allow|deny): <outcome>".

**Phone access** (P9-16; SPEC decision 137; `apps/server/src/remote/tailscale.ts`, `remote/service.ts`, `http/remote-routes.ts`, `apps/desktop/src/phone.ts`, `tray_menu.rs` `phone_menu`). One module holds the Tailscale and Serve logic (find the CLI as a trusted absolute path: `/Applications/Tailscale.app/Contents/MacOS/Tailscale`, then `/opt/homebrew/bin/tailscale`, `/usr/local/bin/tailscale`, and only then the `PATH`, resolved once and logged — the real path, owned by root or the user, neither it nor its folder group/world-writable, answering `version`; `status --json` → `BackendState` and the `*.ts.net` MagicDNS name; `serve status --json` classified as `forwarding_to_us` / `absent` / `taken_by_other` (another target or path, another name's 443, a TCP forward) ; Funnel apart (`funnelOn`: any `AllowFunnel`), so a Serve of ours with Funnel on is still ours to turn off; `remoteHost` added to `config.json` 0600 through a temp file and a rename; `serve --bg --https=443 http://127.0.0.1:<port>` and `serve --https=443 off`; `execFile` with argument arrays, never a shell). `pnpm remote` loads its built copy (`apps/server/dist/remote/tailscale.js`, so the script builds first). The server's `RemoteService` exposes it: `GET /api/remote/status` (`RemoteStatus`: tailscale, name, url, remoteHost state, serve, funnel, serveOwned, turnedOff, ready, restartNeeded, reason + message, remoteClients = remote sockets open now and the last time one was heard, from `WsGateway`; cached 5 s; every client), `POST /api/remote/start|stop` (`requireLocal({always: true})`: the Mac only even with `autoApproveRemote`; refused to an agent's or a run's process by the P9-12 peer guard, 403 `agent_peer`, an unidentified peer allowed and logged; one at a time; a refusal is 409 `remote_refused` with the reason and the status). Start adds `remoteHost` when missing (a running server keeps its loaded config: `restartNeeded`) and starts Serve only when 443 is `absent`, recording `<dataDir>/remote-serve.json` {target, name}; stop turns Serve off only when that record names our handler on `<name>:443` now, Funnel or not (`not_owned` otherwise; "already off" only when 443 is `absent`); the record is removed whenever Serve is seen `absent`. `<dataDir>/remote-off.json` {by: `user` | `outside`} marks phone access turned off on purpose: written by a stop (body `{by: 'user'}`, the default; not `app_quit`, the desktop app quitting with its own server) and when the record's Serve is found absent without our stop (`outside`); `POST /api/remote/start {auto: true}` (the desktop app's automatic start) is refused while it is there, a start without `auto` (the user's) removes it. Check-then-act: Tailscale has no compare-and-set, so a Serve changed between the read and the command is not seen (starts and stops are serialized here). `GALACTIC_PHONE_ACCESS=1` (set by the desktop app on a server it starts while Phone Access is on, never inherited from the app's own environment) adds `remoteHost` before `loadConfig` unless `remote-off.json` is there, giving up after 5 s; the variable is deleted from the server's environment at once so agents and runs do not inherit it. The desktop Node side (`PhoneAccess`) only calls these routes: on a new server, after a reconnect and every 60 s, a `start {auto: true}` when Serve is absent, Tailscale is connected and `turnedOff` is not set; the switch shows `on && !turnedOff`, and turning it on is a user `start`; `stop {by: 'user'}` when it is turned off; on quit, before stopping its own server, `stop {by: 'app_quit'}` (3 s at most); it sends a `phone{on, label, url?, detail?}` line; Rust → Node `phone:on` / `phone:off`. The switch lives in the app's own settings file. The panel polls the status every 10 s while visible (one shared poll per API) for the top bar's indicator and Settings → Access.

**Notifications.** Inside the shell the page's own desktop notifications are off (`window.__GALACTIC_DESKTOP__ = {notifications: 'shell'}`, set by an initialization script; `apps/web/src/desktop-shell.ts`: `notificationApi()` is none, the settings say the app posts them), and the shell posts OS notifications for `notificationOf`'s permission, question, project finished and project failed (the Web Push kinds) — one per id per shell run, never a replay of history (the first snapshot is the baseline); after a reconnect the replay's still-current ones (≤ 1 day, the five most important, then "N more") — decision 59's rules. A click is not routed to the item (the plugin has no click callback on desktop); macOS shows the notification under the app's name. No notification for a full disk (decision 104). The PWA and Tailscale access are unaffected: a phone keeps its own push subscription; a browser on the Mac next to the shell notifies on its own if the user turned that on there.

Commands: `pnpm desktop:build` (everything, then the `.app` under `apps/desktop/src-tauri/target/release/bundle/macos/`), `pnpm desktop:dev`, `pnpm desktop:smoke` (the Node side against a real server in a temp home, no Rust). The TypeScript half is in `pnpm build` / `typecheck` / `lint` / `test`; Rust is only built by the desktop scripts.

### 2.4 Mobile / remote access: PWA over Tailscale Serve

**Recommendation:** make the web UI a PWA (installable, offline shell, push notifications) and expose the local server privately with **Tailscale Serve** (`https://<machine>.<tailnet>.ts.net`).

- Tailscale gives you a private network between your computer and phone, valid HTTPS certificates (needed for PWA, service workers, and WebSockets on iOS), and identity-based access — with **no public port**. It is the option the tunnelling comparison recommends for private dashboards ([source](https://guides.happier.dev/cloudflare-tunnel-vs-tailscale-funnel-vs-ngrok-for-claude-code-codex-opencode)).
- Setup, the remote-detection rule and the trust model of the optional identity headers: `docs/REMOTE_ACCESS.md` (P7-02). Turning it on from the desktop app or the panel, and the status they show: §2.3 "Phone access" (P9-16).
- If you ever need access from a device that cannot run Tailscale, switch to **Cloudflare Tunnel + Cloudflare Access** (SSO in front of the tunnel). Never expose the raw server on a public port: it can start processes on your machine.
- A native mobile app is *not* needed. If it ever is, Tauri 2 can build iOS/Android from the same web UI. The phone must degrade gracefully: lower quality tier, touch controls (one-finger orbit, pinch zoom, tap select), and a "list mode" for when 3D is too heavy (P7-03; SPEC decisions 92–94). `touch-action: none` is set on the canvas and R3F's wrappers only (`.pnl-scene-canvas`), never on the whole scene area, so DOM cards over the scene stay pinch-zoomable (WCAG 1.4.4). Cancelling the canvas's `touchend` (it drops the ghost click) also keeps the focus where it was, so a scene tap blurs a focused text field itself. The slow-connection rule of list mode is skipped on a loopback page (`localhost`, `127.x`, `::1`).
- **The phone's navigation sheet and Back (P8-15; SPEC decision 117).** Android's gesture navigation (One UI on the user's Samsung) takes a swipe from either screen edge as Back before the page sees a touch, and iOS Safari's tab swipe does the same with history behind it, so the sheet's own edge swipe is a convenience and the "Agents" button the primary way. The sheet keeps Back meaningful instead: opening it pushes a history entry of its own (`{navSheet: true}`, same URL); `popstate` while it is open closes it; closed any other way it calls `history.back()` once (a module-level guard against a second back before the `popstate`); and the store's `navigate` *replaces* that entry rather than pushing over it, so Back after a selection made in the sheet goes to the previous selection and Back at the installed app's first entry closes the sheet instead of leaving the app (`apps/web/src/panel/nav-sheet.tsx`, `state/store.ts`).
- **One screen at a time on phones (P8-18; SPEC decision 140).** `panel/phone-view.ts` is a small module store (`'chat' | 'inbox' | 'agents'`), not component state, because the scene's beacons (`revealInbox`, `revealQuestion`) and a notification's click are not React; `usePhoneView(active)` activates it only on the phone panel. Like the navigation sheet it owns a history entry (`{phoneView: true}`, same URL), so Back closes the view; its `popstate` listener is installed once and kept, so a panel unmounting while its own `back()` is under way cannot leave the pending flag set. Opening the sheet while a view is open waits for the view's `back()` before the sheet pushes its entry (`closePhoneViewThen`). The full view (`PhoneFullView`, a modal dialog) is laid over `.pnl-body`; the grid beneath is `inert` and `aria-hidden` but stays mounted, so the conversation, its scroll and the composer's draft survive. The side column's two sections are components of their own (`ManagedAgentsSection`, `InboxSection`), rendered in the aside on wider screens and in the view on a phone.
- **Push notifications on iOS** work only for a PWA installed to the Home Screen, need a user gesture to request permission, and require the server to speak Web Push (VAPID keys, `web-push` library) to Apple's/Google's push service over the public internet. Treat push as best-effort. The service worker must use a versioned, network-first app-shell strategy and never cache API/WebSocket responses.
- **Web Push (P7-04; `docs/tasks/notes/P7-04-push.md`).** Server `apps/server/src/push/`: the VAPID key pair is made on first need in `<dataDir>/vapid.json` (mode 0600, never logged; when it has to be made anew — the file lost or damaged — every subscription written at or before the new key's `createdAt` is cleared with a warning naming only how many, since they are bound to the old key, and `settings.push_keys_cleared_for` records the key cleared for, so a clearing not done before a restart is done after it; `sub` = `https://<remoteHost>`, else a placeholder `mailto:`, `GALACTIC_PUSH_SUBJECT` overrides); devices live in `push_subscriptions` (migration 0010; device configuration, no events), keyed by the first 32 hex digits of SHA-256(endpoint), each with its own switches for the push kinds (`PushKind`: permission, question, project finished, project failed — a subset of `notificationOf`'s) and a `details` opt-in. `PushService` subscribes to the event log, folds each event through `notificationOf` (same ids as the page) and sends with `web-push` (aes128gcm, TTL 6 h for questions, 24 h for projects); 404/410 delete the device, 429/5xx/network retry (≤ 3 attempts, `Retry-After` ≤ 30 s), other answers are recorded as `lastError`; delivery and bookkeeping are separate (P8-12): once the push service accepted a push, a failed record (`last_sent_at`, `lastError`, the removal on 404/410, clearing old subscriptions — any database write) never sends it again; a storage refusal (`isStorageFailure`, §6.1) is logged once per episode, a device whose removal was refused is skipped and its removal retried on the next push (only for that version of its row: one subscribed again since is kept), and subscribing on a full disk is a 507 through the root storage-refusal handler; ≤ 32 in flight (a delivery holds its slot through its retries, so during a push service outage further pushes are dropped — best effort). Routes (bearer-guarded): `GET /api/push`, `PUT /api/push/subscription` (upsert by endpoint; only the browsers' push services — FCM, Mozilla, Apple, WNS — are accepted, so the server never POSTs elsewhere), `POST /api/push/unsubscribe`, `POST /api/push/test` (≤ 1 per 5 s), `DELETE /api/push/devices/:id` (local only unless `autoApproveRemote`). Worker (`sw.js`, after the fetch handler): `push` shows `title` with the notification id as `tag`, the body only when the device chose details (else "Open GalacticAgents to see it."), always `showNotification` even when that tag is already on screen (the same tag replaces it in place with `renotify: false`, keeping the text on screen; Safari on iOS revokes a subscription whose pushes do not show one), a generic notification for a foreign payload (never a silent push); `notificationclick` focuses an open window and posts `NOTIFICATION_CLICK {target}`, else opens `/#focus=<kind>:<id>` (`pwa/notification-clicks.ts` takes it and calls `focusTarget` after the first snapshot). The page's desktop notifications are shown through the registration when there is one (`notifier.ts`), so a push and the page share one tag space and a device shows one notification per id. Page side (`pwa/push.ts`, `PushSettings.tsx`): support detection (iOS outside the Home Screen says so), `pushManager.subscribe` only from the "Turn on" click, `syncPush` on session start hands a rotated subscription back to the server — only when the browser's endpoint differs from the one it remembers (`galactic.push.device` keeps the device id, the endpoint's hash); the same endpoint missing from the server means the device was removed, so the browser forgets its choice and drops the subscription instead of undoing the removal. A lost phone with a valid token can still turn push on again: cutting it off needs a token reset.
- **The service worker is hand-written, not `vite-plugin-pwa`** (P7-01). The shell is one page, a few hashed assets, a manifest and icons; Workbox (what `vite-plugin-pwa` generates) brings a runtime and a config surface far larger than the ~100 lines needed, and its dev-mode and auto-update behaviours would have to be switched off one by one. `apps/web/src/pwa/sw.js` is plain JavaScript; a build-only Vite plugin (`src/pwa/build-plugin.ts`, `apply: 'build'`) copies it to `dist/sw.js` with its `BUILD` slot filled: a version (hash of the build's file names, page and the worker's source) and the precache list (`/`, the entry script, CSS and preloads the built page names, the manifest and icons). Strategy: page navigations network-first, falling back to the cached shell `/` only when the network fails (so a deploy is seen on the next load, never a stale shell while the server answers); `/assets/*` cache-first (content-hashed names); manifest and icons network-first; everything else is never intercepted — `/api`, `/ws`, `/health`, `/dev`, `/debug`, `/files` (one-time downloads, P9-07), non-GET, cross-origin, requests with `Authorization` or `Range`. HTML is kept only as the shell and never under any other name. Each version has its own cache (`galactic-shell-<version>`), older ones are deleted on activation. **Update flow** (`src/pwa/register.ts`): registered in production builds only (`import.meta.env.PROD`; development unregisters any leftover worker, so Vite's HMR is never cached), after `load`, with `updateViaCache: 'none'`; the page re-checks on returning to the foreground and every 30 minutes. A new worker waits; the page asks it (`GET_BUILD` over a `MessageChannel`) whether its precache holds the page's own entry script — if so the page already runs that build and the worker takes over silently (`SKIP_WAITING`), otherwise a "new version — Reload / Later" notice appears and Reload hands over control and reloads once `controllerchange` fires, or once the worker reaches `activated` (a page no worker controls gets no `controllerchange`). "First install" means no active worker (`reg.active`), not no controller: after a hard reload, or before `clients.claim()` lands, a waiting worker is still offered (P7-01 review). Reload on a worker that is no longer waiting (another tab applied it, or it was replaced) reloads plainly. A new worker that goes redundant without activating — a failed install, or a newer one replaced it — is logged (`console.warn`). **Server** (`http/web-cache.ts`): `sw.js`, `index.html` (also the single-page fallback) and the manifest are `Cache-Control: no-cache`; `/assets/*` `public, max-age=31536000, immutable`; icons an hour; a missing file under `/assets/`, `/icons/`, `/sw.js` or the manifest is a 404, never the page. `@fastify/static` runs with `wildcard: true` (one route, a file lookup per request; dotfiles ignored): `wildcard: false` listed the files at startup, so a rebuild without a restart 404'd its new assets — a blank page and a worker whose install failed. Icons are generated by `apps/web/scripts/pwa-icons.mjs` (no dependencies) into `public/icons/`.

---

## 3. The agent adapter layer (mixed CLIs)

This is the most important design decision for a mixed-CLI setup. **Decision (SPEC.md §7, #5):** only Claude Code is implemented on day one, but the communication layer is a runtime-agnostic `AgentAdapter` interface with a fake adapter and a conformance suite, so adding Codex, Gemini CLI or PTY-wrapped CLIs later is one new adapter folder.

A session is owned by exactly **one** transport: an adapter cannot "complement" another adapter's session with a second protocol handle. Each runtime therefore has one adapter, chosen as follows.

### 3.1 Day one: the `claude-sdk` adapter (Claude Agent SDK, TypeScript)

**Stewards, Waykeepers and the Harmonizer all run through the [Claude Agent SDK](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/headless) adapter in v1.** The Harmonizer and Waykeepers already need the SDK for their custom tools, so this is one integration rather than two, and it gives the richest control: hooks, subagent trees (`parent_tool_use_id`), structured output, cost in the final `result`, and resume by session ID (`resume: <session_id>`).

Spawn options (per SPEC §3.5–3.6 and decision 17):

- **Subscription login, not `--bare`.** Bare mode skips CLAUDE.md, hooks, skills, MCP servers and plugins — precisely the repository context a Steward needs — and does not use the subscription login. `bare` is an opt-in per spawn for reproducibility experiments only.
- `cwd` = the Steward's **git worktree** (created by the server under the project's workspace; branch named after the task).
- Permission handling via the SDK's permission callback/hook, which asks the spawn's **permission gate** (below): allowed actions return immediately, denied ones are refused with the reason, everything else becomes `agent.needs_input(kind: "permission")` and blocks the tool call until the user answers.
- Clarification questions: the `AskUserQuestion` tool becomes `agent.needs_input(kind: "clarification")`.
- Graceful stop: `interrupt()` and wait for the turn to end before terminating (SIGTERM alone leaves an in-progress turn and any pending permission unanswered).

*Verified in Phase 1 (P1-08, `docs/tasks/notes/P1-08-sdk-spike.md`):* the SDK bundles its own Claude Code binary and reads the same login as the `claude` CLI, so in-process brains with custom tools run under the subscription; cost is reported per turn as a cumulative estimate (SPEC §8, both answered). What the spike fixed about the adapter's shape:

- **One long-lived `query()` per agent with streaming input.** The inbox (P1-07) is the `AsyncIterable` prompt; each delivered message ends in a `result` (→ `agent.idle`). `query.interrupt()` cancels the current turn only: that turn ends with an error result the adapter maps to `agent.idle`, and the next message runs in the same session. `sessionRef` is the SDK session id, assigned by the adapter (`sessionId`) and re-opened with `resume`.
- **Every tool call goes through the permission gate via a `PreToolUse` hook** (the `canUseTool` callback alone fires only for what Claude Code would prompt for itself). The hook calls `opts.gate.check(toolCall)` and answers `allow`/`deny` from its verdict and `ask` for the rest; `ask` lands in `canUseTool`, which opens `agent.needs_input(kind: permission)` and blocks until the answer. `AskUserQuestion` arrives in the same callback and is answered through `updatedInput.answers` → `agent.needs_input(kind: clarification)`.
- **The gate is the server's, not the adapter's (P1-18).** `SpawnOptions.gate: PermissionGate` — `check(toolCall) → {allow} | {deny, reason} | {ask, action?, reasons}` — is built per spawn by `runtime/permission-gate.ts` from the project's policy table (`domain/permissions.ts`, P1-10) and the agent's caps: the tool-call cap is checked first, and a call refused for the cap also trips it, so the agent is stopped rather than left retrying. Adapters never see the policy or the caps; the ACP adapter's `request_permission` and a PTY adapter's prompt detection call the same `check()`, so every runtime gets the same decisions from one implementation and the policy engine has one caller. The fake adapter runs every tool call through it too. A call the engine cannot classify (an opaque command, an unknown or MCP tool) is decided by the table's `unclassified` entry — `ask` unless the project is `trusted` (SPEC decision 49, §8 below); a push inside such a call still asks.
- **The supervisor spawns the CLI** through `spawnClaudeCodeProcess`, so the SDK session is a child in its own process group with a row in the PID table like any other runtime.
- **`bare` needs an API key** (no subscription login in bare mode); the option is refused without one.

Decided while implementing the adapter (P1-09, `apps/server/src/adapters/claude-sdk/`):

- **A job ends in `agent.idle`, not `agent.finished`.** An SDK session is long-lived: every turn's `result` becomes `agent.idle(cost)` and the session waits for the next message; the Waykeeper (Phase 2) closes the task and stops the agent. `agent.finished` is reserved for the CLI ending on its own (a clean exit, reported by the supervisor as `exited`), `agent.failed(crash)` for an unexpected exit and `agent.stopped` for a completed stop — one terminal event per session, mapped from the supervisor's `ProcessOutcome`. The conformance suite's `jobEnd: 'idle'` describes this.
- **`agent.tool_call` is emitted when the gate lets the call run** (from the `PreToolUse` hook, or from `canUseTool` after the user allowed it), never from the assistant's `tool_use` block, so a refused call is not counted against the tool-call cap and the transcript explains the refusal. Tool results come back as `agent.output(kind: tool)` excerpts; subagent output is prefixed `[subagent]`.
- **Pause is `SIGSTOP` on the process group, resume `SIGCONT`.** The turn in flight — model call, tool, shell children — freezes in place and continues afterwards, which is what the panel's pause means; cancelling the turn would lose work. `stop()` sends `SIGCONT` first, so a paused agent still stops gracefully (interrupt → turn end → SIGTERM → SIGKILL).
- **An error result that is not ours** (`error_max_turns`, an API failure) is a transcript line plus `agent.idle`, not a failure: the session is intact and the next message runs. The error result of our own `interrupt()` is just `agent.idle`.
- **Environment:** the CLI inherits the supervisor's allow-list plus `CLAUDE_PASS_ENV` (API routing and login variables: `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`, `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN`, Bedrock/Vertex settings, …) plus the adapter's own `env`; `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` reaches a child only through that `env`, for `bare`. The session id is assigned by the adapter (`sessionId`), so a server that itself runs under Claude Code never leaks its own session id to its agents.
Decided in the phase review (P1-30, P1-32, P1-36, `docs/tasks/notes/P1-review.md`):

- **One gate check per tool use.** The `PreToolUse` hook's verdict is remembered by `tool_use_id`; `canUseTool` reuses it (asks the user for an `ask`, allows a hook `allow` without a second check), so a call is gated once and has one `agent.tool_call`. Both callbacks deny when the gate throws. `PermissionGate.check(call, {reserve?})` exists for adapters that must re-check without the cap pre-flight.
- **The query loop ending is a failure while the CLI lives.** If the iterator throws (also during an interrupt) or ends and the process is still running with no terminal event after 2 s, the adapter emits `agent.failed(runtime)` and stops the child.
- **Pause needs a process.** Pausing before the CLI exists is refused (the runtime reverts its own paused flag). `interrupt()` while paused sends `SIGCONT` and emits `agent.resumed` first (the runtime resumes first itself). Trade-off of `SIGSTOP`: the CLI's own tool and API timers keep counting while frozen, so a long pause can end the frozen call in a timeout.
- **Mapping:** a `result` with `is_error: true` (also with subtype `success`) is an errored turn — transcript line + `agent.idle`, like the other error results. `AskUserQuestion` with several questions asks them one by one (one `needs_input` each); a multi-select question says "separate the labels with commas" and passes the answer as typed. Cache read/creation tokens are added to `inputTokens` (the protocol `Cost` has one input count). `rate_limit_event` warnings/rejections during a turn become `agent.progress`. An init `session_id` that differs from the resumed ref is reported. Ended sessions are freed; events of a session replaced by a same-agent resume are dropped. An idle stop closes the input, so the CLI exits on stdin EOF before any signal.
- **Brains:** `role !== 'steward'` gets `settingSources: []` (no repository CLAUDE.md, hooks or skills), its `systemPrompt` as the whole prompt, and its `ToolDefinition`s as an in-process MCP server (`mcp__galactic__*`, JSON Schema converted to the zod shape the SDK wants). A Steward keeps the Claude Code preset and the repository's settings; a Steward `systemPrompt` is appended to the preset.

A brain's own tools (`SpawnOptions.tools`) are server code checked by `DomainCommands`: the permission gate allows them by name (bare or `mcp__galactic__<name>`) after the tool-call cap and never consults the policy table for them (P2-06).

**Symlinked workspaces (dev-machine run, 2026-10-02).** Claude Code resolves its cwd, so on a workspace reached through a symlink (macOS `/var` → `/private/var`, a symlinked projects folder) it names resolved paths. The engine stays lexical; the gate passes the resolved spellings of the workspace and the cwd as `PolicyContext.aliases` (`{from: resolved, to: given}`), and both path checks judge a path under `from` as the same path under `to`.

**Permission routing (P2-04).** The gate is the one place a permission is decided. Adapters call `check()` from their hooks; the runtime calls `decide()` (no cap reservation) on every `agent.needs_input(kind: permission)` that carries a tool call, before a question is opened: `allow` / `deny` answer the adapter and log `permission.decided{agentId, questionId?, toolCall (redacted), decision, action?, reason?, decidedBy: system}` with no question row; `ask` opens the user-held question. The gate reads the policy and the workspace on every check (the agent's project's `permissionPolicy` and `workspace`, else the global default and the spawn's; the spawn-time values only for agents outside any project) and logs its own hook refusals as `permission.decided{deny}` without `questionId` (cap refusals are the agent's failure). SPEC decision 31. `decide()` reserves no tool call, because the SDK and fake adapters' hooks already did: a future PTY or ACP adapter that asks the runtime directly, without a hook, must reserve in its permission path, or its allowed calls escape the tool-call cap.

### 3.2 Later: the ACP adapter for other runtimes

[ACP](https://blog.marcnuri.com/agent-client-protocol-acp-introduction) is "LSP for coding agents": JSON-RPC 2.0 over stdio, introduced by Zed in August 2025, adopted by JetBrains and others, with a registry launched in January 2026. Claude Code (`claude-agent-acp`), Codex (`codex-acp`) and Gemini CLI (`--acp`) all speak it, and by early 2026 25+ agents were compatible ([source](https://codex.danielvaughan.com/2026/06/10/agent-client-protocol-microsoft-intelligent-terminal-codex-cli-multi-agent-ide-ecosystem/)). When the second runtime arrives, GalacticAgents becomes an ACP *client*, like an editor, using the official TypeScript ACP SDK:

- `session/new`, `session/prompt`, `session/update` (streamed), `session/cancel` give spawn / talk / observe / stop with one code path; `session/load` (capability `loadSession`) is the resume path.
- **`session/request_permission` is tool authorization**, not a free-form question: map it to `agent.needs_input(kind: "permission")`.
- **Clarification questions arrive via form elicitation** (`elicitation/create`) and only if the client advertises `clientCapabilities.elicitation.form` in `initialize`; without it the agent's question tool is disabled. The adapter must advertise it and handle it → `agent.needs_input(kind: "clarification")`.
- A headless server should not advertise `fs/*` or `terminal/*` client methods unless it implements them faithfully. Document the exact capability set in the adapter README and have the fake ACP agent of the conformance suite assert on it.

*Built in P8-07* (`apps/server/src/adapters/acp/`, `docs/tasks/notes/P8-07-acp.md`), with `@agentclientprotocol/sdk` 1.7.0 (Apache-2.0, the official SDK; ACP protocol version 1):

- **One agent process per session**, started from the configured command (`acp: {command, args?, passEnv?, mode?, allowPermissiveMode?, userSettings?, sessionMeta?}` in `config.json`, or `GALACTIC_ACP_COMMAND`) through the supervisor's `adopt()` like the Claude CLI: own process group, PID table, `GALACTIC_AGENT_ID` marker, the graceful stop (cancel the turn → close stdin → SIGTERM → SIGKILL); whatever the supervisor records before a fork (P8-11's launch intent) covers it too. The `acp` runtime is registered only when a command is configured. The process's fate is the session's single terminal event, as for the SDK adapter. A job ends in `agent.idle` (`jobEnd: 'idle'`): the session waits for the next prompt.
- **The capability set:** `initialize` advertises `clientCapabilities: {elicitation: {form: {}}}` and nothing else — no `fs/*`, no `terminal/*` (the agent uses its own tools in its own cwd, the Steward's worktree; its tool calls still reach the gate), no `elicitation.url`, no `auth.terminal`. The fake ACP agent refuses a client that advertises otherwise, so the conformance run asserts it.
- **Permissions.** `session/request_permission` → the call is translated into the policy engine's vocabulary (`gateCall`: `execute`+command → `Bash` — prefixed `cd '<dir>' && ` when the input names a `cwd`/`workdir` —, `read` → `Read`, `edit` → `Edit`, `delete` → `Bash rm -r -- '<path>' …` (judged as the shell command is: `destructive`, and `outside_workspace` when it leaves the workspace — not an `edit_workspace`), `move` → `Edit` with both paths, `search` → `Glob` (a file-name pattern, located by the engine) unless it is plainly a content search (`Grep`: grep-only fields, a `glob`, a grep/rg title, or only a `query`), `fetch` → `WebFetch`; every distinct path a call names — its path fields, a `paths` list, every location — is judged, the engine reading a `paths` array like the single path fields, so the strictest wins; anything that cannot be placed stays unclassified and so `ask`s unless the project is trusted, and the engine adds `push` to an unknown tool whose input or title mentions one (`mentionsPush`, decision 49) so a trusted project still asks) and checked with `gate.check()` — which also reserves it against the tool-call cap, as §3.1's permission-routing note requires of an adapter without a hook; a call already judged when it started is checked with `reserve: false`, so it is counted once: `allow` answers the `allow_once` option and emits `agent.tool_call`, `deny` answers `reject_once`, `ask` opens `agent.needs_input(kind: permission)` with the call attached (the runtime's `decide()` sees the same `ask`, so the user gets the card). The adapter never answers with an `*_always` option while a `*_once` exists, so every later call still reaches the gate.
- **Isolated settings** (decision 108 amendment, 2026-10-07). `session/new` and `session/load` carry `_meta` (`sessionMetaFor`): by default `{claudeCode: {options: {settingSources: []}}}` — `claude-agent-acp` passes `_meta.claudeCode.options` through to the Agent SDK, overriding its own `settingSources: ["user", "project", "local"]`, so no filesystem settings load (no user or repository allow/deny rules, hooks, skills, plugins, `CLAUDE.md`) and the calls its default mode deems risky reach `session/request_permission`, i.e. the table, before they run — plus `allowDangerouslySkipPermissions: false` (the bypass mode is gone from its catalog) unless `acp.allowPermissiveMode`. `acp.userSettings: true` drops the isolation (the agent's own settings, as interactive Claude Code); `acp.sessionMeta` is merged deeply over the defaults, for another agent's session options or a narrower opening (`settingSources: ["project"]`). `[]` rather than `["project"]`: the Steward's worktree is the user's repository, and its `.claude/settings.json` can hold allow rules and hooks (shell commands) that would act before the table; Stewards get their task context from their prompt. Agents that do not read `claudeCode` ignore it (ACP `_meta` is an extension point); for them nothing here controls their settings, which the settings caveat says. `claude-agent-acp` itself still reads the user's settings for its *starting* mode and model (its own settings manager, not the session's sources): a permissive starting mode is refused as below, so set `acp.mode` if `~/.claude` names one. Verified against the real agent: isolated, `git add` (a user allow rule) and `rm -rf` (a user deny rule) both came as permission requests; with `userSettings`, the first ran unasked and the second was refused by the agent.
- **Session modes** (decision 108). The `session/new` / `session/load` response's `modes` (or a `configOptions` select of category `mode`) are read; `acp.mode` (by id or name) is set with `session/set_mode` (or `session/set_config_option`) before the first prompt — a mode the agent does not list fails the spawn. A session left in a known permissive mode (`PERMISSIVE_MODES` in `mapping.ts`: `bypassPermissions`, `dontAsk`, `acceptEdits`, `yolo`, `autoEdit`, `full-access`, `auto` and the auto/accept-all variants, matched on id or name, any case) fails the spawn with the reason unless `acp.allowPermissiveMode: true`. An agent without modes is allowed. That mode is the expected one; `current_mode_update` (or the mode config option changing) to another mode, or a `switch_mode` tool call run without asking, opens a hold.
- **Calls the agent runs without asking** (its own permission mode allowed them) are checked when they start — `in_progress`, `completed` or `failed`, whichever comes first (a call reported `failed` is taken as one that ran, unless the agent marks it as refused before running: `claude-agent-acp`'s permission-denial report, a `failed` update with `_meta.claudeCode.toolResponse.decisionReasonType`, which it sends for a call its own rules, classifier or mode denied — nothing ran, so nothing is judged, held or reported; the denial is in the transcript; real run, P8-07) — and judged once more (`reserve: false`) if their raw input or locations change afterwards: `allow` → reported; `ask` → reported and a **hold**; `deny` → the Steward is stopped (`agent.failed`, reason `runtime`, the reason as its error; the call is not counted), so it shows as needing the user's attention. A **hold** cancels the turn (`session/cancel`) and opens `agent.needs_input(kind: permission)` — "the agent ran X without asking; …let it continue?" with the call attached (so the runtime's `decide()` re-judges it), or "the agent switched itself to mode X; …" with no call (always the user's). The cancelled turn ends without `agent.idle`: the agent waits on the answer. Allowed → a new turn tells the agent to carry on (or `agent.idle` when no turn was running; an allowed mode becomes the expected one); refused → the Steward is stopped as for a `deny`; an interrupt withdraws the hold → `agent.idle`. One hold at a time; a mode change seen meanwhile is checked once it is answered. The ACP agent cannot be held before such a call: cancelling the turn and stopping the agent are the strongest responses available.
- **Environment.** The agent gets the supervisor's allow-list plus `ACP_PASS_ENV` — routing variables only (base URLs, the Bedrock/Vertex switches, region, profile; proxies are in the allow-list) — plus what `acp.passEnv` names: every credential (`CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN`, `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`/`ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN`, `GEMINI_API_KEY`, `GOOGLE_*` credentials, `OPENAI_API_KEY` …) must be named there. `GALACTIC_*` names are refused (config load and adapter). `GALACTIC_ACP_COMMAND` is a command line split on whitespace (no quoting) or a JSON array of strings (`["/opt/my agent/acp", "--flag"]`).
- **Clarifications:** `elicitation/create` in form mode → one `agent.needs_input(kind: clarification)` per field (required fields first; the message is the question, an enum or `oneOf` the options, a boolean yes/no, a multi-select comma-separated); the answers go back as `accept` with typed values; an interrupted question is answered `cancel`. An optional free-text field right after a choice field is that choice's "Other" box (`claude-agent-acp` sends `question_<n>` + `question_<n>_custom`, nothing required): it is not asked on its own, and an answer that names none of the choices goes there instead; an answer matches a choice by title or value, or when it names exactly one of them ("the first option" → `First option`). The tool call an elicitation names (`toolCallId`: the agent's question tool, `AskUserQuestion`) is not judged as an unasked call when the table cannot place it (`acp:*`): the user's answer is its whole effect. A call the table can place is judged as usual even then.
- **Resume:** `sessionRef` = `acp:<the agent's session id>`; `resumeSession` starts a new process and calls `session/load` when the agent advertises `loadSession`, ignoring the conversation the agent replays while loading, then sends the resume prompt. Without `loadSession` the resume fails and the agent stays `interrupted` with its restart action (as §3.3 says for PTY).
- **Cost:** dollars from `usage_update.cost` (the session's cumulative total; USD only, another currency stays unreported), tokens added up from each prompt response's `usage`. An agent that reports neither leaves `cost` off: budgets show "cost not reported" (P8-01). A resumed session's agent may restart its own tally: the spend fold keeps the higher figure per session, so such a session can be under-counted until it passes its earlier total.
- **Trace:** `GALACTIC_ACP_TRACE=1` (stderr) or `=<file>` logs every JSON-RPC line both ways and the agent's stderr, credential-looking string values (`token`, `key`, `secret`, `password`, `authorization`, `cookie`) redacted (`acp/trace.ts`); for diagnosing a real agent, off by default.
- **Ordering:** the SDK settles a response at once but runs notification handlers through an async chain, so the turn's last `session/update`s can still be in flight when the prompt's response arrives; the adapter ends the turn one macrotask later.
- **Runtime selection** (decision 109): the `stewardRuntime` setting (`config.json`, `GALACTIC_STEWARD_RUNTIME`, the settings page; live) names the runtime brains spawn new Stewards on; absent = the brain's own runtime. Only a registered runtime is accepted, and `fake` only in dev mode (`GALACTIC_DEV`); the settings page offers the registered runtimes `GET /api/runtimes` lists (never `fake`) and says that an ACP agent may run a tool call before the permission table judges it (decision 108). The Harmonizer and Waykeepers stay on the Claude SDK: their server-side tools would need an MCP bridge over ACP, so the ACP adapter refuses a spawn with `tools`. The ops console's spawn form already offers every registered runtime per spawn (`GET /api/runtimes`).
- **Verified against `claude-agent-acp` 0.86.0** (2026-10-07, a Claude Team login; `acp-real.conformance.test.ts` with `GALACTIC_REAL_ACP="claude-agent-acp"`): 22/22 cases green, nothing skipped. What it does: advertises `loadSession` (resume works), reports dollars on `usage_update.cost` at each turn's end and tokens on the prompt response, offers modes `default` (Manual) / `acceptEdits` / `plan` / `auto` / `bypassPermissions` (it starts in `default`; `acceptEdits`, `auto` and `bypassPermissions` are in `PERMISSIVE_MODES`), asks with `AskUserQuestion` through a form elicitation (the choice plus its "Other" box, see Clarifications), and announces tool calls as `pending` with an empty `rawInput`, filling the input in later updates; a call it runs without asking usually goes straight from `pending` to `completed` (no `in_progress`). By default it loads the Claude login's own settings (`--setting-sources=user,project,local`): calls those settings allow run without asking, calls they deny are refused by the agent itself before running (reported `failed` with its denial marker; nothing for the table to judge). Since the decision 108 amendment the adapter asks for isolated settings (above), so this happens only with `acp.userSettings`. Notes: `docs/tasks/notes/P8-07-acp.md` ("Real runtime run").

### 3.3 Fallback: PTY adapter for anything else

For CLIs with no machine protocol, spawn them under `node-pty`, stream the terminal to the UI (xterm.js in the side panel), and derive status heuristically (output activity, exit code, prompt detection). Treat this as "observable but dumb": no structured permission requests, so questions must be detected by regex/LLM and surfaced as best-effort. A PTY agent cannot be resumed after a restart; it is shown as `interrupted` with a "restart task" action.

*Built in P8-08* (`apps/server/src/adapters/pty/`, `docs/tasks/notes/P8-08-pty.md`), with `node-pty` 1.1.0 (MIT; prebuilt for macOS and Windows, compiled from source at install on Linux — allowed in `pnpm.onlyBuiltDependencies`) and `@xterm/xterm` 6.0.0 (MIT) in the web app:

- **One CLI per session**, from `config.json` `pty: {command, args?, passEnv?, idlePattern?, questionPatterns?, clarificationPatterns?, quietMs?, idleAfterMs?}` (or `GALACTIC_PTY_COMMAND`, parsed like `GALACTIC_ACP_COMMAND`); the `pty` runtime is registered only when configured, and `node-pty` loads at the first spawn. The CLI goes through the supervisor's `adopt()` with a new **`fork` hook** on `ProcessSpec` (`pty-child.ts`: the PTY wrapped in the `ChildProcess` subset the supervisor uses — `stdout` is the terminal, `stdin.write` types, `stdin.end()` is Ctrl-D, `exit` then `close`), so it gets the launch intent (P8-11), the PID-table row, `GALACTIC_AGENT_ID`, the graceful stop and the group reaping like every agent process; `forkpty` makes it a session leader, so its pid is its process group. Environment: the supervisor's allow-list, `TERM=xterm-256color`, `COLORTERM`, and only what `pty.passEnv` names (credentials by name; `GALACTIC_*` refused). The terminal is a fixed 120×32 (`PTY_TERMINAL_SIZE`), which the panel's view uses too. `node-pty` 1.1.0 ships its macOS `spawn-helper` without the execute bit; the adapter adds it before loading (`ensureSpawnHelper`) and the preflight reports a helper it cannot fix.
- **Who may run one** (decision 112). A terminal agent's tool calls are invisible to the permission table, so each spawn (and each later message, and each clarification answer before it is typed) asks the gate about the session as a whole — an unclassified call `pty:session {command, cwd}` (the configured command line, placeholders unexpanded), not reserved against the cap — and runs only when the table allows what it cannot classify (the `trusted` preset, or a custom table with `unclassified: allow`); a command line mentioning a push still asks (decision 49), and its refusal says so. A refused spawn fails with the reason; a refused message or answer ends the running agent: `agent.failed(runtime, "the project's permission table no longer allows terminal agents")` and its process stopped, so the inbox does not keep retrying. The settings page says so where the Steward runtime is chosen.
- **The prompt is typed**: once the CLI is ready (its idle prompt is on the screen, or the output has been quiet for `quietMs`, at the latest after 15 s), the first prompt is typed as one line and Enter; every later message likewise. For a Steward the first prompt carries its role instructions ahead of the task (what other runtimes receive as a system prompt): the CLI reads them as ordinary input, not as a system prompt of its own. Every typed line goes through `oneLine()`: newlines become spaces and every other control character — C0 but TAB (Ctrl-C, Ctrl-U, ESC and so the escape sequences it starts), DEL, C1 — is dropped, on every path (first prompt, messages, answers, the user's line); `POST /api/agents/:id/terminal` refuses them (400 with the reason, `TerminalInputBody`).
- **Long lines.** A terminal in canonical (line) mode keeps about 1024 bytes of a line (macOS `MAX_CANON`) and drops the rest. A first prompt or message over 1000 bytes is written to `<cwd>/.galactic/pty/<session>-<n>.txt` (directory 0700, file 0600; `.galactic/` is already excluded from git through `info/exclude`) and the line typed is `Read your instructions from <path>`; the files are removed when the session ends. `{promptFile}` in `pty.args` puts the first prompt in such a file and its path on the command line instead (the session starts in its first turn, nothing typed). A `{prompt}` placeholder (the text itself as an argument) was not added: arguments are stored in the launch intents (P8-11) and visible to `ps`. Answers and the user's own line answer a prompt, so they are typed as they are; one over 1000 bytes gets a warning line in the transcript.
- **Output: two transcript forms.** The raw output, escapes kept, becomes `agent.output{kind: 'terminal'}` batched every 100 ms or 8 KB — a batch that is only carriage-return redraws (a spinner: `\r`, no newline) is held up to 1 s, so a spinner costs about a row a second — — a new `TranscriptChunk` kind (no migration: the chunk is JSON) that only the terminal view reads; the same output with escapes stripped and `\r` redraws collapsed (`TerminalText`) becomes `text` blocks of lines for the conversation, history and search. Storage roughly doubles for a PTY agent; retention (P8-02) compacts both. The transcript pipeline keeps the `terminal` stream apart from the others (its own boundary carry and private-key state), so interleaving the two forms does not weaken the split-secret redaction. The first line of each turn, then one line every 30 s, becomes `agent.progress`.
- **Status, heuristically.** Output is work, silence past the PTY stall threshold (2 min) is `stalled` (the status store, unchanged). After `quietMs` of silence the cursor's line is read (`detect.ts`): `idlePattern` (the CLI's input prompt) ends the turn with `agent.idle`; every question **fails closed** to a **permission**-kind question without a tool call — so neither a brain nor the table can answer it (P2-04: a request without a call goes to the user) — unless a `pty.clarificationPatterns` regex matched the line (then a **clarification** whose answer is typed; a yes/no confirmation stays a permission even then). On a yes/no prompt (`[y/N]`, `(yes/no)`, aider's `(Y)es/(N)o [Yes]:`, `yes/no [no]:`, `[Yes]/[No]`, "Continue?", "Delete …?", "Do you want to …?", or a configured `pty.questionPatterns` shape with its own `yes`/`no`) "allow" types its yes token (`y`; `yes` when the prompt spells the pair out, except aider's which takes `y`), anything else its no; on any other question (a line ending in `?`) "allow" presses Enter (the CLI's default) and anything else interrupts the turn (Ctrl-C) — the card says so, and a worded answer is typed from the Mac. With `idleAfterMs`, that much silence also ends a turn (a CLI without a recognisable prompt). A question the CLI moved on from (more lines followed it) is still answered, but nothing is typed and the transcript says so. Exit code 0 → `agent.finished` (its last lines as the result), another code → `agent.failed(runtime)` with the last line, a signal → `agent.failed(crash)`.
- **Controls.** Interrupt = Ctrl-C; the turn ends when the idle prompt returns (or the output goes quiet when there is no `idlePattern`), at the latest after 3 s. Pause = SIGSTOP/SIGCONT on the group (output held, nothing emitted). Stop = the supervisor's sequence with Ctrl-C, the turn's end and Ctrl-D as the hook, then SIGTERM, SIGKILL.
- **No resume, no cost.** `resumeSession` is absent: after a restart the agent stays `interrupted` (startup recovery flags it, as for any agent it cannot reopen). The user's action on it (`POST /api/agents/:id/recover`, "Restart from its first prompt" in the panel) starts it afresh from its saved first prompt in the same working directory — `recoverAgent()` takes the fresh path for a runtime without `resumeSession`; startup never does. No cost is reported: budgets say "cost not reported".
- **The terminal view and the user's keyboard.** The Steward panel shows a PTY Steward's `terminal` chunks in xterm.js at 120×32, read-only (`disableStdin`; scrolled sideways on a narrow panel), its text transcript folded below. A line can be typed into the terminal (`AgentAdapter.input`, optional; `AgentRuntime.typeInto`; `POST /api/agents/:id/terminal`) — at once, whatever the turn is doing, for a prompt the detection missed — logged as the user's `message.sent` (text through `redactSecrets`; the input's help says not to type passwords) + `message.delivered`; only from the Mac's own browser, refused (403 `remote_control`) for a remote client even with `autoApproveRemote` (decision 113). Typed while the agent is idle, the line opens a turn in the adapter (the screen is read for questions again) and the runtime marks the agent busy (`idle = false`, the inbox `working()`), so a queued message waits for the turn's end. The view keeps the last 512 KB of terminal output (trimmed to half past that, saying "Earlier output trimmed"), and links a CLI prints (OSC 8) open through an explicit `linkHandler`: http(s) only, after a confirmation, in a new window with `noopener` — the same in a browser and in the desktop app (whose navigation guard rate-limits what it hands to the default browser).
- **Conformance:** the suite runs against `fake-pty-agent.sh` (bash under a real PTY, in CI) with the new target options `toolCalls: false` (no `agent.tool_call` expected; the permission cases check `permissionEvidence` in the transcript instead), `spawnPolicy` (trusted) and the new optional cases `policyDeny` and `toolCalls` skipped: 19 cases green, 2 skipped, resume cases not run (`resumable: false`).

### 3.4 One internal event model

Every adapter emits the same normalised events (schemas in `packages/protocol`) into the server's event bus:

`agent.spawned · agent.output(text/tool/thinking/terminal) · agent.tool_call · agent.needs_input(questionId, kind, question: QuestionContext{text, options?, toolCall?, permissionAction?}) · agent.progress(summary) · agent.idle · agent.stalled · agent.paused · agent.resumed · agent.finished(result, cost) · agent.failed(error) · agent.stopped`

The `AgentAdapter` interface: `spawn(opts)`, `send(sessionId, message, {interrupt?})` (starts a turn now — the server's per-agent inbox is what waits for idle, so adapters keep no queue and may reject a mid-turn message; P1-19), `answer(questionId, answer)`, `dismiss?(questionId, reason)` (P9-14), `interrupt`, `pause`, `resume`, `stop`, `resumeSession?(sessionRef)`, `input?(sessionId, text)` (a terminal runtime's typing, P8-08), `commands?(sessionId)` (the session's slash commands, P8-13), plus the `events` stream. `spawn(opts)` accepts `tools` and `systemPrompt` so the same adapter runs the brains, and `gate` (§3.1) for its tool calls. An adapter is a translator, not a scheduler: the runtime owns the inbox, the caps, the permission policy and the event log.

The UI, the status store and the supervisor brains only ever see these events. Adding a new runtime means adding one adapter, nothing else — including for the brains, which never import a runtime module directly.

Two refinements decided while building the fake adapter and the supervisor (P1-01, P1-03):

- **`agent.spawned` is emitted by the server's runtime layer**, not by adapters, when `spawn()` / `resumeSession()` resolves: it carries the body, supervisor, task and project ids the adapter never sees, plus the `sessionRef` from the handle — written to the agent row in the same transaction (P1-21). **Every other `agent.*` event comes from the adapter, the one emitter for its session** (P1-20). The process supervisor tells the adapter what only it can know as a `ProcessOutcome` through the spec's `onOutcome` hook — `start_failed`, `crashed`, `exited` (clean, or announced with `expectExit()`), `stopped{by}`, `restarting` — and the adapter decides the verdict (`agent.failed(reason: runtime)`, `agent.failed(reason: crash)`, `agent.stopped`, or `agent.finished` from the runtime's own final message), so a process exit and a final result can never race each other into the log.
- **One terminal event per agent (P1-17).** The runtime logs exactly one of `agent.finished` / `agent.failed` / `agent.stopped`: a cap stop is flagged on the live entry and the adapter's `agent.stopped` is folded into `agent.failed(reason: cap)` whichever arrives first; a straggler from an ended agent is dropped; an event for an agent the runtime never spawned is reported and never logged. The status store treats an ended record as a sink.
- **`agent.needs_input` opens the question (P1-23).** The runtime inserts the `Question` row and emits `question.opened` in the same command as the event; `answer()` closes the row and emits `question.answered` with the bumped `version`. The server owns questions (§5.3); holder routing arrives with Phase 2.
- **`agent.created{agent}` describes the row** (emitted by whichever command inserts it: the Phase 1 runtime, the Phase 2 `spawnAgent` command), so a replaying client builds the same `agents` table as a snapshotting one (§6.2); `agent.spawned` then reports the session. `agent.idle` may carry the running `cost` for runtimes that meter per turn.
- **Cost reports are cumulative per session (P8-01 review).** The `cost` on `agent.idle`, `agent.finished` and `agent.failed` is always the session's running total, never one turn's: the spend fold (`foldSpend`) keeps the highest figure per session and sums the sessions. An adapter whose runtime meters per turn accumulates before it reports; a resumed session continues its own total. Part of the `AgentAdapter` contract (`packages/protocol/src/adapter.ts`).
- **Adapters emit `agent.output` per content block, never per token**: one transcript row per token is not a transcript. The pipeline's redaction (§6.2, §8) is boundary-aware since P1-27, so a key split across two deltas no longer leaks — but the per-block rule stands for the sake of the rows. The runtime redacts every string in the other agent events too (tool inputs, question text, results, errors, progress summaries) before they reach the log.
- **`agent.paused` / `agent.resumed` / `agent.stopped` emitted by an adapter carry `by: {type: system}`**: the adapter interface has no actor. Who asked is recorded by the matching `control.*` event the runtime appends first (decision 11b), so the log always has both.
- **Slash commands are runtime state, not events (P8-13; notes `docs/tasks/notes/P8-13-14-composer.md`).** `commands?(sessionId): Promise<SlashCommand[]>` returns what the session's runtime last said can be typed as `/name` at the start of a message — `SlashCommand{name, description?, argumentHint?, aliases?, builtin?}` in `packages/protocol/src/slash.ts`, passed through `normalizeSlashCommands` (no leading `/`, one-word names and aliases — the SDK's `aliases`, e.g. `/cost` for `/usage`, are kept and match like the name — one-line bounded text, one row per name, at most 500). The `claude-sdk` adapter reads `query.supportedCommands()` once the CLI reports `system/init`, caches it per session and replaces the cache on a `system/commands_changed` push (a call that fails is reported and asked again later; a slow one gives `[]` after 5 s); the ACP adapter keeps the last `available_commands_update` (`input.hint` → `argumentHint`; kept during a `session/load` too); the fake adapter is scripted (`commands` option, a scenario's `commands`; the development server's fake runtime reports `DEV_FAKE_COMMANDS`); the PTY adapter has none. Nothing is logged: `AgentRuntime.commands(agentId)` reads the adapter (`[]` without a live session) and remembers a non-empty answer per live session for `hasCommand(agentId, name)` (name or alias); `GET /api/agents/:id/commands` returns it (`AgentCommandsResponse`; read-only, remote clients too). **A brain's list is an allowlist (P8-13 review; SPEC decision 115):** for the Harmonizer and a Waykeeper, `commands()` keeps only `BRAIN_SLASH_COMMANDS` — `compact`, `context`, `cost`, `usage`, the runtime's own rows (`brainSlashCommands`) — so the endpoint, the composer's list and the runner's `hasCommand` agree; `/clear` (it would reset the session behind the runner's back and break the saved `sessionRef`), `/init`, `/review`, `/security-review` (a Steward's work) and every skill stay text. A Steward keeps its runtime's whole list (its permission table governs what it does). A brain's session has its commands read by the runtime as soon as it opens (spawn, resume, reset), and until that read returns a list `hasCommand` takes the allowlist as offered by any runtime that has `commands` — so a command sent right after a restart still runs; an empty answer is not remembered (adapters give `[]` for "not known yet"). A message whose first word is `/name` is sent unchanged, so the runtime runs the command; a brain's runner (§5.2) sends such a user message as a turn of its own. **A local command's answer reaches the transcript:** Claude Code runs `/context`, `/cost` and the like without the model and replays their output as a user message wrapped in `<local-command-stdout>` (or `-stderr`); the `claude-sdk` adapter emits it as `agent.output` (text, tags stripped), and a successful turn that put no text in the transcript emits its `result` text — the shape is what the bundled CLI names, still to be confirmed by a real run (notes). The text is agent- and plugin-author-provided: the page renders it as text only.
- **An adapter may stop a session on its own account and wait on the user between turns (P8-07 review; decision 108).** The ACP adapter ends a session it stops itself (a call the agent ran without asking that the table denies, a hold the user refused) with `agent.failed{reason: 'runtime', error: <the reason>}` rather than `agent.stopped`, so the Steward shows as failed with the reason. A *hold* — a permission question the adapter opens after cancelling the turn (a call run without asking that the table would have asked about, a mode switch) — keeps the cancelled turn from ending in `agent.idle` until it is answered: an `agent.idle` with a question open withdraws the question (P1-34), and the agent is waiting on the user, not on its next message.

**Restart recovery (P1-14, P1-15, P1-29, P1-32, P1-33; SPEC decisions 8, 21, 23).** `agent.interrupted{agentId, reason, pending?}` is the runtime's event for a session the server lost without the agent ending. A graceful shutdown first logs every live agent's fate — `agent.interrupted{pending}`, or the `agent.stopped{by}` / `agent.failed(cap)` a stop already under way was heading for — then flushes the transcripts, then stops all processes in parallel (the server's hard deadline is grace + term + 10 s). A start after a crash of the server logs `agent.interrupted` for every agent whose last state is still live, or `agent.stopped` when a `control.stop` after its last spawn was never answered. The status store folds `interrupted` to an ended state that only a new `agent.spawned` leaves. `AgentRuntime.recover()` reads the log once and reopens every interrupted agent through `recoverAgent(id)` — the same path the panel's "Resume session" (`POST /api/agents/:id/recover`) takes: refused unless the agent is `interrupted` and not live; the permission policy, workspace, prompt and `bare` come from `Agent.meta` (until tasks carry them in Phase 2); every `message.sent` without a `message.delivered` is queued again with its original id, so a second restart before delivery still knows it is pending and a crash mid-turn does not deliver the turn's message twice. A failed resume logs `agent.interrupted{reason: "resume failed: …"}`. An agent recovery leaves interrupted for the user (passed over, not resumable, or a resume that failed for good) also gets `recovery.needs_attention{agentId, reason}`, once per interruption (P25-01; the notification feed's "work interrupted by a restart").

**Message and question lifecycle (P1-33, P1-34).** `message.delivered{messageId, agentId}` is logged when the adapter's `send()` resolved; a refused delivery waits at the head of the inbox and is retried at the next idle (three attempts). A delivery marks the agent busy at once, so a pause/resume or a second message right after cannot reach a mid-turn session. An agent's open questions are cancelled (`question.cancelled{questionId, agentId, reason, version}`, row status `cancelled`) in the same command as its terminal or interrupted event, and when it goes idle with a question still open (an interrupt withdrew it); a late answer is refused. A stop whose adapter call throws ends the agent with `agent.failed(runtime)`; a stop that resolved without an adapter event ends with the runtime's `agent.stopped{by}` — an agent can always be stopped. A message sent while a stop is under way is refused before it is logged. A cap hit before the session handle exists is acted on when it arrives.

**Dismissed questions (P9-14, SPEC decision 135).** `POST /api/questions/:id/dismiss {version?}` → `DomainCommands.dismissQuestion({questionId, by: user, version?, origin})`: checked like an answer (`checkDismiss`: a stale `version`, an answered question — "already answered by …" — or a withdrawn one — "already withdrawn" — is 409 `stale`; an answer to a withdrawn question is refused the same way, so a late answer from the offline queue or the menu bar reads "already withdrawn"; an agent never dismisses, it withdraws its own with `withdrawQuestion`). A question a live session waits on goes through `AgentRuntime.dismiss` — the adapter first, then the log, like an answer (decision 99 refuses the user's dismissal whole on a full disk) — and any other (a brain's tool-asked question, a harness question) is cancelled in the log alone. Either way the record is `question.cancelled{…, by, origin?}` with the reason `dismissed by the user` (`DISMISSED_BY_USER`); `by` is what makes a cancellation a dismissal: the row folds `meta.cancelledBy` / `meta.cancelReason` (server and `foldWorld` alike, `dismissalOf()` reads it), the status store treats it like an answer (the agent goes on working), and the brain runner lets it through to the asking brain's own mailbox (any other `question.cancelled` naming the brain stays its lifecycle). The wake line tells the asker "the user dismissed q-… ("text") without answering — do not wait for an answer: carry on with what you know, or ask again if it still matters", and a former holder that it "needs no answer from you any more". The adapters' `dismiss`: the Claude SDK adapter resolves the pending `canUseTool` — an `AskUserQuestion` call is refused (`behavior: deny`) with that text as the tool result and the call's later questions are not asked, a permission request is denied ("The user refused: the permission request was dismissed by the user"); ACP declines an elicitation (`action: decline`), answers a permission request `reject_once` and refuses a hold (the agent is stopped, as Refuse does); PTY types the prompt's "no" (or interrupts a free-form prompt) for a permission and types nothing for a clarification, only a transcript note — its `dismiss` resolves `false` (the agent is not unblocked: the prompt is still up), the runtime records `stillWaiting: true` on the `question.cancelled`, and the status store keeps the agent `waiting_for_input` until its output says it moved on; the fake adapter prints `dismissed: …` or skips the call. An adapter without `dismiss` gets `answer()` with a refusal or the reason as text. **Supersede**: after `POST /api/agents/:id/send` logs the user's message (not a slash command), `Commands.supersedeQuestions(agentId)` withdraws that agent's own open clarifications the same way with `by: system` and the reason `superseded by the user's message` (`SUPERSEDED_BY_MESSAGE`) — skipping permission requests, `meta.toUser` questions (decision 30) and every question of the Harmonizer; the non-live ones are cancelled in the same tick as the message, so a brain's runner folds both into one turn; an adapter error on one question is logged and the others are still withdrawn. Never queued offline: the panel disables the × while the stream is down. Remote clients may dismiss (decision 90): `origin` is on the event.

The **process supervisor** (`apps/server/src/runtime/supervisor.ts`) is the one place that spawns and kills child processes: each in its own process group (killed by group, corner case 4), stdio captured, an allow-listed environment (§8), restart policy off by default, and one graceful stop sequence — the runtime's interrupt hook (SIGINT to the group by default) → wait for the turn to end with a timeout → SIGTERM → SIGKILL (corner case 8b). Its PID table is the `agent_processes` table (migration 0003): runtime bookkeeping that never appears in a snapshot and has no events. On startup `reconcile()` treats every `running` row as a previous server life: a still-running orphan is killed by group (nothing can re-attach to a live process, corner case 3), and both dead and orphaned rows become `interrupted` for the resume path (P1-14) to respawn from `agents.sessionRef`.
